--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of the scores:**
- Champion: 10029.90 (significantly better than 2nd place)
- 2nd/3rd: ~10087.80 (nearly identical to pure Stop behavior!)
- 4th: 12437 (pure Exploration)
- 5th+: 13125+ (various moving behaviors)

**Key insight:** The mission rewards being stationary before the switch and moving after. Score ~10087 for pure Stop (1200 steps × 20 robots × ~0.42 ≈ ~10000 for first phase being still, then penalized for not moving after switch). The champion at 10029 is slightly better than pure Stop.

The champion's structure: State 0 = Repulsion (moving), State 1 = Stop (magenta LED), State 2 = Stop (cyan LED). The logic seems to keep robots moving initially (repulsion spreads them out, reducing stationary count? No wait...).

Actually wait - re-reading: **before switch, score increases for stationary robots. After switch, score increases for moving robots.** So optimal strategy: ALL robots MOVE before switch, ALL robots STOP after switch. 

But pure Stop scores ~10087 (roughly 20 robots × 500 steps = 10000 for second phase when they should move). The champion at 10029 is slightly better.

The champion has robots starting in Repulsion (moving = good before switch), then transitioning to Stop states. The transition from Stop back to moving could be triggered by seeing green light (the signal). The champion transitions from Stop states back to Repulsion on seeing floor colors or neighbor counts.

**Optimal design:** Move before switch (exploration/repulsion), detect green wall light after switch → Stop.
- State 0: Exploration/Repulsion (moving, pre-switch)
- State 1: Stop (post-switch, triggered by seeing green=color 2?)

Wait, green wall lights up at switch time. Color 2 = green in the clr parameter? Let me use GoToColor with green or detect color 7 condition with green.

**Strategy for round 2:**
1. Survival: copy champion
2. Exploit champion topology with tweaks
3. Explore: Move-then-stop triggered by green light detection (C=7, l=2 for green)

FSM designs:
- 2-state: Move → Stop on seeing green light
- 3-state variants of champion with tweaked params
- Pure stop variants
- Move + stop triggered by neighbor count changes

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations:**
- Champion (8028.95): 2-state, Exploration (rwm=20) → Stop, triggered by seeing green (color 2, p=0.9) or random (p=0.002). Stop state rarely escapes (p=0.001).
- 2nd (8372.44): Similar but uses Repulsion instead of Exploration in state 0.
- 3rd (8666.88): 3-state with two Stop states, similar green-detection trigger.
- Large gap between 3rd (8666) and 4th (9613) - the simple 2-state green-detection topology is clearly best.

**What the champion does:** Robots explore (move) until they see the green light (the wall that lights up at switch time), then stop. This is nearly optimal! Before switch: moving (good). After switch: stop when green detected (good).

**Why it's not perfect:** The green detection probability 0.9 means some robots don't immediately stop. The random escape p=0.002 from Stop means some robots accidentally leave Stop state. Also, not all robots may see the green wall clearly.

**Exploitation targets:**
1. Tweak rwm (turning rate) - champion uses 20, try 10, 30, 50
2. Tweak green detection probability - try 0.95, 0.99, 1.0
3. Tweak random escape from Stop - try 0.0005, 0.001, 0.002
4. Add neighbor-count condition to help robots that can't see green
5. Try Repulsion instead of Exploration (2nd place was close)
6. Try adding a second transition from Stop back to moving (in case green disappears from view)

**Key insight:** The stop state transition back (p=0.001) is almost never fired - that's fine, we want robots to stay stopped. The main lever is how quickly robots detect green and stop.

**Exploration ideas:**
- What if robots start stopped and begin moving only after seeing green? (Last place showed this is bad at 13273)
- 3-state: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop (approach then stop near green wall)
- Add condition: if many neighbors (clustered), also stop

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from rounds 1-3:**
- The winning topology is clear: 2-state FSM, State 0 = Exploration (moving), State 1 = Stop
- Transition from State 0 → State 1: triggered by seeing green (color 2, C=7, high probability) + small random escape
- Transition from State 1 → State 0: tiny random probability (rarely fires, keeps robots stopped)
- Champion: rwm=10, p_green=0.9, p_random=0.002, p_stop_exit=0.001

**Trends:**
- Lower rwm is better (10 > 15 > 20 > 30 > 40): slower turning = more straight-line movement = better coverage = more robots see green
- Green detection probability: 0.9 seems good, 0.95/0.99 slightly worse (maybe too eager to stop on false positives?)
- Repulsion with rep=2.0 scored 7963 (close to champion) - repulsion spreads robots to see green faster
- The stop-exit probability should stay very low

**What to try:**
1. **Exploitation:** rwm=5, rwm=7, rwm=8 (go lower than 10), tweak p values
2. **Repulsion variants:** rep=1.0, 1.5, 2.0 with rwm=10
3. **Try rwm=1** (nearly straight movement - maximum coverage)
4. **Add neighbor-based transition** to stop state (many neighbors → staying clustered → might miss green)
5. **Try p_green = 0.85** (slightly lower, less false positives in pre-switch phase)
6. **Combination:** Start with Attraction to cluster, then detect green to stop

The core insight: lower turning rate (rwm) keeps robots moving in straighter paths, covering more area and making it more likely they face the green wall when it lights up. Rep=2.0 also spreads robots which helps coverage.

# ROUND 5/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key finding:** rwm=1 (nearly straight-line movement) is dramatically better (6110 vs 6791 for rwm=5). This makes sense: robots moving in straight lines cover the arena faster and are more likely to face the green wall when it lights up. The near-straight trajectory means each robot sweeps a wide arc of view.

**Parameter analysis:**
- rwm=1 >> rwm=5 > rwm=7 > rwm=10 (clear monotonic trend, lower is better)
- p_green=0.9 seems optimal (0.95 slightly worse in some cases)
- p_random_move=0.002 seems good for state 0 escape
- p_stop_exit=0.001 very low keeps robots stopped
- Repulsion variants are worse than pure Exploration with rwm=1

**What to try for round 5:**
1. **Survival:** Keep champion (rwm=1, exact copy)
2. **Exploitation:** Tweak p_green (0.8, 0.85, 0.95, 0.99), p_random (0.001, 0.003, 0.005), p_stop_exit
3. **Can we go lower than rwm=1?** No, 1 is the minimum integer.
4. **Try Repulsion with rwm=1 analog:** Repulsion moves in straighter lines when there are no neighbors
5. **3-state exploiting rwm=1:** Start with rwm=1, detect green → stop; stop → detect green again to confirm
6. **What about GoToColor(green)?** After detecting green, go toward it then stop
7. **Add neighbor-based stop:** If isolated (no neighbors), robot might be at edge near green wall

The main question now: can we squeeze below 6110? The champion is nearly optimal for the move→stop strategy. Key remaining variables:
- p_green detection: currently 0.9, try 0.8 (less likely to false-stop before switch?) 
- The random exit p=0.002 from state 0: this means ~0.2% chance per step to stop randomly, which is also good (some robots stop early)
- Stop exit p=0.001: keep very low

Let me also think about whether GoToColor could help: after the green wall lights up, robots move toward it, making them more likely to stay stopped near the wall. But GoToColor is moving, which is penalized after switch...

Actually the key insight: after the switch, robots need to STOP. GoToColor would make them move toward green, which is penalized. So Stop is correct after detecting green.

# ROUND 6/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key finding:** The stop-exit probability (p_stop_exit) is the most critical parameter! 
- p=0.0001 → 5099 (champion!)
- p=0.0005 → 5628
- p=0.001 → 6189
- p=0.003 → 6521
- p=0.005 → 7209

Lower p_stop_exit = better! Once robots stop, they should almost never randomly restart. The champion has p=0.0001 (1 in 10000 chance per step to accidentally restart).

**Other findings:**
- p_random from state 0 (p0x1): 0.002 is optimal (0.001 worse, 0.003 worse, 0.005 worse)
- p_green=0.9 remains best
- rwm=1 is confirmed best
- The 3-state variant (rank 3, 5728) with two stop states and occasional re-check of green is also promising

**What to try for round 6:**
1. **Survival:** Champion (rwm=1, p_green=0.9, p_random=0.002, p_stop_exit=0.0001)
2. **Can p_stop_exit go even lower?** Try 0.00005, 0.00001 (essentially never restart)
3. **Tweak p_random (state 0 → stop randomly):** Try 0.0015, 0.0025
4. **Tweak p_green:** Try 0.88, 0.92
5. **3-state exploiting low p_stop_exit:** Both stop states with very low exit probability
6. **What if p_stop_exit = 0?** Can't do 0 exactly (would need no transition from stop), but... actually we need --n1 >= 1. Let's try p=0.00001.
7. **No transition from stop:** Wait, we MUST have --n<I> >= 1. So minimum 1 transition. Try p=0.00001.

The key insight: the stop-exit probability being essentially 0 means once a robot stops after seeing green, it stays stopped forever. This is nearly optimal because after the switch, we want all robots stopped.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Critical finding:** The champion now has p_stop_exit = 0 (exactly zero)! Once a robot stops after seeing green, it NEVER restarts. Score: 5087.39.

**Analysis of the cluster around 5251:**
- p_stop_exit=0.0001 gives ~5251 (worse than 0)
- p_stop_exit=0 gives 5087 (best)
- The scores 5251.51, 5251.61, 5251.68 are nearly identical regardless of p_green (0.88, 0.9, 0.92, 0.95) or state count

**What's the remaining source of error?**
The score 5087 with 20 robots over 1200 steps:
- Before switch (~500 steps avg): robots moving = 0 penalty per moving robot. Score from moving robots = 0. But wait - robots might stop prematurely due to p_random=0.002 from state 0!
- After switch (~700 steps avg): stopped robots = 0 penalty. But robots that haven't seen green yet are still moving.

The p_random=0.002 from state 0 causes some robots to stop early (before switch) - this is BAD (adds to pre-switch penalty). But it's also the only way robots stop if they never see green.

Actually wait: before switch, moving = GOOD (0 penalty), stationary = BAD (+1 per step). After switch, moving = BAD, stationary = GOOD.

So the p_random transition from Explore→Stop (p=0.002) causes robots to stop during the pre-switch phase, which is PENALIZED. We want this to be 0 ideally!

**What if p_random = 0?** Robots only stop when they see green. Before switch, no green → all move → 0 penalty. After switch, green appears → robots that see it stop → those that don't keep moving → penalty for non-stopping.

But we need at least one transition from state 0. If p_random=0, that transition effectively never fires. Let's try p=0.0001 or even 0.0 for the random transition.

Wait - the champion has p_random=0.002 and p_stop_exit=0. Let me think about whether reducing p_random helps:
- With p=0.002, each robot has ~0.002×500=1 expected premature stops before switch → ~1 step stopped × 20 robots = 20 penalty steps pre-switch
- But actually it's more complex: once stopped with p=0, they stay stopped forever!

So p_random=0.002 means robots can stop permanently BEFORE the switch if they randomly trigger! This is very bad. We should minimize p_random.

**Key insight:** p_random should be as small as possible (or 0). But we need at least 1 transition. Try p=0.0001, 0.00001, 0.0.

Also: what if we use a neighbor-based condition for state 0 exit instead of random? Less likely to false-trigger.

## Reasoning

**Critical finding from round 7:**
- p_random=0.0001 (from state 0) gives ~7072 - MUCH WORSE than p=0.002 (5113)!
- p_random=0 gives 7490 - also worse than p=0.002
- p_random=0.001 gives 5257 - close to champion
- p_random=0.002 gives 5113 (this round) and 5087 (champion, previous run)

**Paradox resolved:** The random transition from Explore→Stop (p=0.002) is actually HELPFUL, not harmful! Why? Because even before the switch, some robots stopping early isn't that costly (they're stationary, which costs during pre-switch phase), BUT without the random transition, robots that never face the green wall directly will NEVER stop after the switch either! The green wall only covers one side - robots facing away from it won't detect it. The random stop ensures eventual coverage.

Wait, let me reconsider: p=0.0001 gives ~7072 and p=0 gives 7490. So the random exit from state 0 IS important because some robots never see green and need another way to stop.

**Key parameters confirmed:**
- rwm=1 (minimum turning, straight movement)
- p_green=0.9 (green detection probability)
- p_random_stop=0.002 (critical for robots that don't face the green wall)
- p_stop_exit=0 (never leave stop state)

**What's left to optimize?**
1. The champion is stable at ~5087-5113 (run variance). 
2. Try p_random_stop between 0.001 and 0.003 more finely
3. Try different p_green values with p_random=0.002, p_exit=0
4. Try adding a second condition to help robots detect green more reliably
5. Consider: what if robots in stop state can detect green and use it to confirm staying stopped?

The scores 5087 vs 5113 may just be noise. Let me fine-tune around the sweet spot.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Current champion:** p_random=0.0015, p_green=0.9, p_stop_exit=0, score=4941.36

**Round 8 analysis:**
- p_random=0.0015 → 4941 (best)
- p_random=0.0018 → 5009
- p_random=0.002 → 5019
- p_random=0.0022 → 5043
- p_random=0.0025 → 5263
- p_random=0.0012 → 5279

The sweet spot is around 0.0015. The scores are very close (4941-5043 for p in [0.0015, 0.0022]) suggesting we're near a plateau. The p_green variations (0.85-0.95) all give ~5019-5029, so 0.9 is fine.

**What's left to squeeze?**
The remaining score ~4941 with 20 robots × 1200 steps = 24000 total robot-steps.
- If switch is at step 500 avg: pre-switch penalty = (stationary robots) × 500 steps. With all robots moving, pre-switch penalty ≈ 0 except for random stops.
- Post-switch penalty = (moving robots) × 700 steps. Robots that never see green and don't randomly stop contribute here.

With p_random=0.0015 per step: Expected steps before random stop ≈ 667 steps. So a robot not facing green will stop around step 667 on average. If switch is at 500, that means ~167 steps of post-switch penalty for that robot.

**Fine-tuning p_random around 0.0015:**
Try 0.0013, 0.0014, 0.0016, 0.0017

**Alternative: Can we do better with a different second condition?**
What if instead of random (C=5), we use a neighbor-based condition? If a robot has many neighbors (clustered near walls), it might be near the green wall. But this seems unlikely to help.

**What about p_green?** The scores for 0.88-0.95 are all very similar (~5019-5029). The main driver is p_random.

**New idea:** What if state 0 has TWO transitions: green detection (high prob → stop) AND random (low prob → stop)? Actually that's what the champion already has. The 3-transition version with duplicate green detection scored 5020 (slightly worse due to interference).

Let me focus on fine-tuning p_random around 0.0015 and also try some genuine exploration.

## Reasoning

**Final round analysis:**

The champion (4941.36) is stable: rwm=1, p_green=0.9, p_random=0.0015, p_stop_exit=0.

Round 9 results show:
- p_random=0.0015: scores range 5041-5054 (slightly worse than champion's 4941 - run variance)
- p_random=0.0013: 5061
- p_random=0.0014: 5066
- p_random=0.0016: 5091
- p_random=0.0017: 5301
- p_random=0.0019: 5414

The sweet spot is clearly at 0.0015. The champion's 4941 vs round 9's 5054 for same params is just noise.

**p_green analysis this round:**
- 0.85 → 5041 (best this round!)
- 0.88 → 5048
- 0.9 → 5054
- 0.92 → 5053
- 0.95 → 5053

Interesting - lower p_green (0.85) was best this round. This might mean lower false-positive stops are more important. But this is within noise range.

**Final strategy for round 10:**
- Survival: champion
- Fine-tune p_random around 0.0015 (try 0.00145, 0.00155)
- Fine-tune p_green around 0.85-0.9 (try 0.82, 0.83, 0.84, 0.86, 0.87)
- Try combining lower p_green with p_random=0.0015
- A few exploratory machines with slightly different structure

The key insight for this last round: the champion has p_green=0.9 but this round p_green=0.85 gave 5041. Let me try the combination of p_green=0.85 with p_random=0.0015, as well as very fine-grained search around these values.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
